Built pretrained transformer encoder architecture, GLiNER2 maintains CPU efficiency and compact size while introducing multi-task composition through an intuitive schema-based interface. Our experiments demonstrate competitive performance across extraction and classification tasks with substantial improvements in deployment accessibility compared to LLM-based alternatives. We release GLiNER2 as an open-source pip-installable library with pre-trained models and documentation at https://github.com/fastino-ai/GLiNER2.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GLINER2 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GLINER2, an enhanced version of the GLINER architecture, designed for efficient multi-task information extraction (IE) on CPU. GLINER2 unifies named entity recognition (NER), text classification, and hierarchical structured data extraction within a single model. It uses a schema-driven interface for intuitive task composition and maintains a small parameter count (under 500M) for easy deployment in resource-constrained environments. The authors demonstrate GLINER2's competitive performance against LLM-based alternatives on standard IE benchmarks, emphasizing its CPU efficiency and accessibility. The library is released as an open-source, pip-installable package with pre-trained models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Efficiency and Accessibility:** The primary strength is the focus on CPU efficiency. By achieving competitive performance without requiring GPUs, GLINER2 significantly lowers the barrier to entry for organizations and researchers with limited resources or privacy concerns. This is a practical and valuable contribution.
*   **Multi-Task Unification:** Consolidating NER, text classification, and hierarchical extraction into a single model simplifies deployment and reduces the need for maintaining multiple specialized models. The schema-driven interface enhances usability.
*   **Open Source and Practical Release:** The release of GLINER2 as a pip-installable library with pre-trained models demonstrates a commitment to usability and reproducibility, making it immediately accessible to the NLP community.
*   **Clear Performance:** GLINER2 demonstrates good zero-shot performance on a variety of benchmarks, exceeding open-source baselines.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Core Architecture:** The paper builds heavily on the existing GLINER architecture. The core innovation seems to be the extension of the prompting strategy and schema-based extraction for multi-task learning, which, while practically useful, may not represent a significant theoretical breakthrough.
*   **Benchmarking Against Open-Source Models:** While outperforming other open-source alternatives is commendable, it is important to note that open-source models usually don't scale up and usually lag behind commercial models in certain datasets, therefore it might not be fully comparable with those models.
*   **Limited Hierarchical Extraction Evaluation:** The lack of evaluation for the hierarchical structure extraction task due to the absence of established benchmarks is a notable limitation. Demonstrating performance on this key feature would strengthen the paper.
*   **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** The model relies heavily on synthetic data which is a common practice, but the GPT-4 based automated annotation might lead to specific biases, therefore the generated data might not fully reflect the characteristics of real-world data.
*   **Marginal improvements:** Given its dependence on GLINER, the presented work can be viewed as having marginal improvements over it, especially after the adaptation of GLINER to other subdomains of Information Extraction.

**Significance and Potential Impact:**

The significance of GLINER2 lies in its practical utility. While not groundbreaking in terms of architectural innovation, it provides a valuable tool for efficient and accessible information extraction. Its ability to run effectively on CPUs makes it suitable for a wide range of applications where GPU resources are limited or privacy is a concern. It helps democratize access to advanced NLP capabilities. While it builds on existing work, its focus on a specific application space (efficient IE) and its practical release give it a good chance of being adopted.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**. While GLINER2 offers practical benefits and has demonstrable utility, its architectural novelty is relatively limited. The lack of hierarchical extraction benchmarks is a noticeable gap. However, the emphasis on CPU efficiency, the unified architecture, and the practical release as an open-source library are significant strengths that warrant a score above average. The contribution isn't revolutionary, but evolutionary and useful. The paper adds to the growing trend of efficient NLP models and addresses a specific need within the IE community.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### VideoMind: An Omni-Modal Video Dataset with Intent Grounding for Deep-Cognitive Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18552v1)
- **Authors**: Baoyao Yang, Wanyun Li, Dixin Chen, Junxiang Chen, Wenbin Yao, Haifeng Lin
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces VideoMind, a video-centric omni-modal dataset designed for deep video content cognition and enhanced multi-modal feature representation. The dataset comprises 103K video samples (3K reserved for testing), each paired with audio and systematically detailed textual descriptions. Specifically, every video and its audio is described across three hierarchical layers (factual, abstract, and intent), progressing from surface to depth. It contains over 22 million words, averaging ~225 words per sample. VideoMind's key distinction from existing datasets is its provision of intent expressions, which require contextual integration across the entire video and are not directly observable. These deep-cognitive expressions are generated using a Chain-of-Thought (COT) approach, prompting the mLLM through step-by-step reasoning. Each description includes annotations for subject, place, time, event, action, and intent, supporting downstream recognition tasks. Crucially, we establish a gold-standard benchmark with 3,000 manually validated samples for evaluating deep-cognitive video understanding. We design hybrid-cognitive retrieval experiments, scored by multi-level retrieval metrics, to appropriately assess deep video comprehension. Evaluation results for models (e.g., InternVideo, VAST, UMT-L) are released. VideoMind serves as a powerful benchmark for fine-grained cross-modal alignment and advances fields requiring in-depth video understanding, such as emotion and intent recognition. The data is publicly available on GitHub, HuggingFace, and OpenDataLab, https://github.com/cdx-cindy/VideoMind.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the VideoMind paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VideoMind, a new video-centric, omni-modal dataset designed to facilitate deep cognitive video understanding. The key innovation lies in its comprehensive, hierarchical textual descriptions of video content, ranging from factual observations to abstract summaries and, crucially, speculated intents.  VideoMind includes 103K video samples (3K for test), along with audio data, and detailed textual descriptions, generated using a Chain-of-Thought (COT) prompting approach with an mLLM. The dataset includes annotations like subject, place, time, event, action, and intent. The authors also establish a manually validated benchmark of 3,000 samples for evaluating deep-cognitive video understanding. They demonstrate the dataset's utility through hybrid-cognitive retrieval experiments, assessing several baseline models and highlighting the limitations of existing models in capturing video intent. The dataset is made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The most significant novelty lies in the addition of the *intent* layer to the textual descriptions. Existing video datasets typically focus on factual descriptions and, to some extent, abstract summarization.  The VideoMind dataset explicitly attempts to infer the *intent* behind the video and the actions of its main characters. This is a crucial aspect of video understanding that has been largely overlooked in previous datasets.  The use of COT prompting to generate these intent-focused descriptions is also a valuable contribution.  The dual-role-playing task within the COT framework adds another layer of sophistication, aiming to reduce ambiguity and improve accuracy in intent speculation. While others have incorporated audio information and ASR/OCR, the systematic organization and in-depth annotation of multiple modalities, coupled with the intent-driven analysis, distinguishes VideoMind.
The manually validated benchmark adds credibility and allows for more meaningful evaluation of future models on a challenging cognitive understanding task.

**Significance:**

The VideoMind dataset addresses a critical gap in video understanding research. By explicitly incorporating intent, it pushes models beyond simple object recognition and activity detection towards a more nuanced and human-like understanding of video content.  This has potential applications in various areas, including:

*   **Improved video retrieval:**  Retrieving videos based on intent, rather than just keywords or object descriptions.
*   **Better video summarization:**  Summaries that capture the purpose and motivations behind the video.
*   **More accurate video understanding systems:**  For safety, copyright, and content appropriateness assessment.
*   **Enhanced multimodal learning:** Providing rich textual annotations to better align different modalities (video, audio, text).

The experiments included in the paper demonstrate the limitations of existing pre-trained models when dealing with the intent layer, further highlighting the need for a dataset like VideoMind. The release of the dataset will enable the research community to develop new models and techniques for deep cognitive video understanding.
**However, there are some limitations to consider:**

*   **Subjectivity of intent:** Inferring intent is inherently subjective. While the authors have taken steps to minimize ambiguity, there will inevitably be some level of interpretation involved in the intent descriptions. This may introduce bias into the dataset. The risk is mitigated by double-validation from expert annotators for the test set.
*   **Generalization limitations:** The dataset's diversity (45 countries, 24 categories) is important, but whether the models trained on VideoMind can generalize well to unseen categories or significantly different video styles remains to be seen.
*   **Computational cost:** Generating the deep textual descriptions using COT prompting is computationally expensive. This may limit the scalability of the dataset creation process.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper.

*   **Strengths:**  The novel addition of intent annotation, the use of COT prompting, the creation of a validated benchmark, the comprehensive multi-modal approach, and the public release of the dataset are all significant strengths. The paper clearly articulates the limitations of current datasets and provides a convincing case for the need for VideoMind.
*   **Weaknesses:** The potential subjectivity of intent and the limitations of generalization are valid concerns. More information is needed on how the team dealt with different intents of various subjects.

The VideoMind dataset makes a significant contribution to the field of video understanding, providing a valuable resource for developing more intelligent and human-like video understanding systems. Its emphasis on intent will likely inspire further research in this area.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### The Geometry of LLM Quantization: GPTQ as Babai's Nearest Plane Algorithm
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18553v1)
- **Authors**: Jiale Chen, Torsten Hoefler, Dan Alistarh
- **Abstract**: Quantizing the weights of large language models (LLMs) from 16-bit to lower bitwidth is the de facto approach to deploy massive transformers onto more affordable accelerators. GPTQ emerged as one of the standard methods for one-shot post-training quantization at LLM scale. Yet, its inner workings are described as a sequence of ad-hoc algebraic updates that obscure any geometric meaning or worst-case guarantees. In this work, we show that, when executed back-to-front (from the last to first dimension) for a linear layer, GPTQ is mathematically identical to Babai's nearest plane algorithm for the classical closest vector problem (CVP) on a lattice defined by the Hessian matrix of the layer's inputs. This equivalence is based on a sophisticated mathematical argument, and has two analytical consequences: (i) the GPTQ error propagation step gains an intuitive geometric interpretation; (ii) GPTQ inherits the error upper bound of Babai's algorithm under the no-clipping condition. Taken together, these results place GPTQ on firm theoretical footing and open the door to importing decades of progress in lattice algorithms towards the design of future quantization algorithms for billion-parameter models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "The Geometry of LLM Quantization: GPTQ as Babai's Nearest Plane Algorithm":

**Summary:**

The paper establishes a mathematical equivalence between GPTQ (a popular one-shot quantization method for large language models) and Babai's Nearest Plane algorithm, a classical algorithm for solving the closest vector problem (CVP) in lattice theory.  Specifically, the authors prove that when GPTQ is executed in reverse order (from last to first dimension) on a linear layer, it's mathematically identical to applying Babai's algorithm to a lattice defined by the Hessian matrix of the layer's inputs. This connection provides a geometric interpretation for GPTQ's error propagation step and allows the authors to import Babai's error bound guarantees into the context of GPTQ, providing a theoretical foundation for the method. The paper further explores the role of quantization order and proposes a "min-pivot" heuristic to improve it.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The connection between GPTQ and Babai's algorithm is a significant and non-trivial finding.  GPTQ, while empirically successful, has lacked a strong theoretical justification for its effectiveness. Framing it within the well-studied context of lattice theory and CVP algorithms is a novel and insightful perspective. The connection offers a new lens through which to understand and potentially improve GPTQ. The "min-pivot" quantization order is a useful, albeit incremental, addition.

* **Significance:** This work is significant because it bridges a gap between the practical world of LLM quantization and the theoretical world of lattice algorithms. This bridge opens up the possibility of applying decades of progress in lattice algorithms to the development of new and improved quantization techniques for LLMs. It provides a way to analyze GPTQ's performance limitations and potentially design algorithms with better worst-case guarantees.  By casting GPTQ as an approximation to a well-known hard problem (CVP), it suggests that further improvements may require adopting more sophisticated approximation techniques from lattice theory.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Mathematical Foundation:**  The paper is mathematically rigorous, providing a detailed proof of the equivalence between GPTQ and Babai's algorithm.
    * **Geometric Interpretation:** Provides a clear and intuitive geometric interpretation of GPTQ's error propagation step.
    * **Import of Existing Theory:**  Leverages existing theory (Babai's error bound) to provide a formal guarantee for GPTQ's layer-wise quantization error (under the no-clipping condition).
    * **Practical Implications:** The identification of a new quantization ordering heuristic which can be used to improve GPTQ.
    * **Clear Writing:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the technical details.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The analysis focuses primarily on the no-clipping condition. While this provides a valuable starting point, the more practical scenario of quantized weights requires further investigation.  The no-clipping condition limits its direct applicability to the most common low-bit quantization scenarios.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** The proposed "min-pivot" heuristic, while theoretically motivated, may not lead to substantial empirical improvements in all cases. Further experimentation is needed to assess its practical impact.
    * **Complexity:** The mathematical details might be challenging for readers without a background in lattice theory and optimization.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of future quantization algorithms for LLMs by providing a theoretical framework based on lattice algorithms.  It could inspire researchers to explore and adapt more sophisticated lattice-reduction techniques or other CVP solvers for LLM quantization.  The work could also lead to a better understanding of the limitations of current quantization methods and guide the design of algorithms with better trade-offs between accuracy and computational efficiency.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper is mathematically sound and establishes a novel connection, its practical implications are currently somewhat limited by its focus on the no-clipping condition and incremental improvement with "min-pivot". The significance lies more in opening a new avenue for theoretical analysis and potential future development than in directly offering a significantly superior quantization technique today. The mathematics requires some background to appreciate. It is definitely a major step in analyzing the GPTQ algorithm, that can lead to improvements and a more robust algorithm.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### HARLF: Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning and Lightweight LLM-Driven Sentiment Integration for Financial Portfolio Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18560v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Coriat, Eric Benhamou
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a novel hierarchical framework for portfolio optimization, integrating lightweight Large Language Models (LLMs) with Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) to combine sentiment signals from financial news with traditional market indicators. Our three-tier architecture employs base RL agents to process hybrid data, meta-agents to aggregate their decisions, and a super-agent to merge decisions based on market data and sentiment analysis. Evaluated on data from 2018 to 2024, after training on 2000-2017, the framework achieves a 26% annualized return and a Sharpe ratio of 1.2, outperforming equal-weighted and S&P 500 benchmarks. Key contributions include scalable cross-modal integration, a hierarchical RL structure for enhanced stability, and open-source reproducibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HARLF: Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning and Lightweight LLM-Driven Sentiment Integration for Financial Portfolio Optimization" presents a novel hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL) framework designed to optimize financial portfolios.  It integrates sentiment analysis derived from financial news (using lightweight LLMs like FinBERT) with traditional market indicators. The framework employs a three-tier architecture: base RL agents process raw data (financial metrics and sentiment), meta-agents aggregate decisions from the base agents, and a super-agent synthesizes cross-modal signals to determine the final portfolio allocation. The authors train the system on historical data (2003-2017) and backtest it (2018-2024), comparing its performance against equal-weighted and S&P 500 benchmarks.  The results indicate the framework outperforms these benchmarks, achieving a 26% annualized return and a Sharpe ratio of 1.2. Key contributions highlighted include cross-modal integration, hierarchical aggregation, and practical applicability through the use of lightweight LLMs. The code is made publicly available through Google Colab notebooks for reproducibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its *hierarchical architecture* for integrating LLM-derived sentiment with traditional financial data within an RL framework. While individual components like sentiment analysis with FinBERT and RL for portfolio management are not entirely new, the combination and specific hierarchical arrangement are. The framework's ability to adaptively combine decisions from different data modalities based on market conditions, through meta-agents and a super-agent, shows some advance. However, some points require further validation:
    *   **Incremental Nature:**  The use of hierarchical RL and sentiment analysis integration is not entirely groundbreaking. Similar ideas have been explored in other domains. The degree of novelty is therefore dependent on how significantly this framework advances the state-of-the-art in *financial* applications specifically. The authors need to provide a clearer and more detailed comparison against existing financial applications of HRL and LLM integration, highlighting *exactly* where their approach diverges and offers an advantage.
    *   **Lightweight LLM Justification:** The emphasis on lightweight LLMs (e.g., FinBERT) is a positive aspect, especially considering latency constraints in financial applications.  However, the paper doesn't fully explore *why* this is beneficial *compared to using larger LLMs and what specific optimizations were performed*. There is a mention that it outperforms larger models like GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 in financial sentiment classification tasks but it would be helpful to include comparison within the portfolio optimization framework and not just in the sentiment analysis framework.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Performance Improvement:** The reported performance (26% annualized return, Sharpe ratio of 1.2) is significant, if robust. However, it is important to provide a more detailed assessment.
        *   **Statistical Significance:** The paper should include statistical significance tests (e.g., t-tests, Sharpe ratio tests) to demonstrate that the improvements over benchmarks are statistically significant and not due to random chance.
        *   **Robustness:** The backtesting period (2018-2024) is relatively short and includes the COVID-19 pandemic, which may have skewed results. The paper needs to discuss the potential limitations of this backtesting period and address how the framework might perform under different market conditions.  It is particularly important to analyze the stability of the framework and sensitivity to regime shifts.
        *   **Transaction Costs:** the authors should include transaction costs to see how the performance of the framework changes.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The provision of Google Colab notebooks is a major strength, promoting transparency and enabling others to replicate and build upon the work.
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  The use of lightweight LLMs makes the framework more practically relevant for latency-sensitive applications. The hierarchical structure also enhances interpretability, which is crucial in finance.

**Score & Justification:**

Score: 6/10

Justification:

The paper presents a promising HRL framework for portfolio optimization that integrates LLM-derived sentiment. The hierarchical structure, cross-modal integration, and use of lightweight LLMs are valuable contributions. However, the paper's novelty is somewhat limited by the incremental nature of the approach, and the performance claims require further statistical validation and robustness analysis. The limited backtesting period and the omission of transaction costs are also weaknesses. The provision of reproducible code is a significant strength. A higher score could be justified if the authors provide a more detailed comparison with existing methods, a more comprehensive statistical analysis of the results, and a more thorough discussion of the framework's limitations and robustness. Finally, a discussion of the code optimizations to make it fit inside a Google Colab environement would strengthen the paper even further.

- **Classification**: q-fin.PM
- **Score**: 6/10

### Adversarial Distribution Matching for Diffusion Distillation Towards Efficient Image and Video Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18569v1)
- **Authors**: Yanzuo Lu, Yuxi Ren, Xin Xia, Shanchuan Lin, Xing Wang, Xuefeng Xiao, Andy J. Ma, Xiaohua Xie, Jian-Huang Lai
- **Abstract**: Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) is a promising score distillation technique that compresses pre-trained teacher diffusion models into efficient one-step or multi-step student generators. Nevertheless, its reliance on the reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence minimization potentially induces mode collapse (or mode-seeking) in certain applications. To circumvent this inherent drawback, we propose Adversarial Distribution Matching (ADM), a novel framework that leverages diffusion-based discriminators to align the latent predictions between real and fake score estimators for score distillation in an adversarial manner. In the context of extremely challenging one-step distillation, we further improve the pre-trained generator by adversarial distillation with hybrid discriminators in both latent and pixel spaces. Different from the mean squared error used in DMD2 pre-training, our method incorporates the distributional loss on ODE pairs collected from the teacher model, and thus providing a better initialization for score distillation fine-tuning in the next stage. By combining the adversarial distillation pre-training with ADM fine-tuning into a unified pipeline termed DMDX, our proposed method achieves superior one-step performance on SDXL compared to DMD2 while consuming less GPU time. Additional experiments that apply multi-step ADM distillation on SD3-Medium, SD3.5-Large, and CogVideoX set a new benchmark towards efficient image and video synthesis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adversarial Distribution Matching (ADM), a novel framework for distilling diffusion models into efficient image and video generators. Unlike Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) which relies on reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence (potentially leading to mode collapse), ADM leverages diffusion-based discriminators to align latent predictions between real and fake score estimators in an adversarial manner.  For one-step distillation, the paper proposes Adversarial Distillation Pre-training (ADP) using hybrid discriminators in both latent and pixel spaces, and incorporates a distributional loss on ODE pairs from the teacher model for better initialization.  Combining ADP with ADM fine-tuning results in a unified pipeline called DMDX. Experiments show superior one-step performance on SDXL compared to DMD2 and better multi-step distillation results on SD3 and CogVideoX, setting new benchmarks for efficient synthesis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of replacing the explicit divergence metric in DMD-based methods with a learned adversarial discriminator is a notable contribution.  It addresses a known limitation of predefined divergence metrics in capturing the complexities of high-dimensional distributions. The hybrid latent and pixel space discriminators in ADP are also a practical and effective addition for stabilizing one-step distillation. The cubic timestep schedule adds a useful refinement.

*   **Significance:** The paper's results demonstrate significant gains in distillation performance, particularly in one-step image generation. This acceleration enables new levels of efficiency in diffusion-based synthesis and video generation. Setting new benchmarks on widely used models and datasets like SDXL, SD3, and CogVideoX highlights the practical significance of the work. The focus on reducing GPU memory consumption is important, as this accelerates adoption.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined adversarial distillation framework.
    *   Thorough experimental validation across various models and datasets.
    *   Effective combination of different components (ADP, ADM, hybrid discriminators).
    *   Addressing mode collapse and instability issues in distillation.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical connection between the adversarial loss and total variation distance, while interesting, could be expanded. While it shows minimization of TVD, it doesn't fully explain *why* TVD leads to the performance boost in distillation over KL divergence. Are there specific properties of TVD that are especially well-suited for the score matching loss in this specific application?
    *   Some of the claims, such as about the causes for the success of TVD over reverse KL, are only partially justified by the theory.  The paper depends partly on experimental evidence for support of such claims.
    *   The method has many moving parts (ADP, ADM, pixel-space discrim, latent-space discrim, timestep schedule) - While each are justified and useful, a deeper analysis of how they interact with each other and whether some could be simplified or are more impactful than others would benefit the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is likely to have a significant impact. The adversarial approach to distribution matching in distillation provides a promising direction for future research and development of more efficient and robust diffusion models. The method's practical improvements in synthesis speed and memory efficiency will be valuable for both researchers and practitioners.
**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and effective method for distilling diffusion models, backed by solid experiments. The adversarial distribution matching idea, with its hybrid latent/pixel space discriminators, overcomes limitations of previous divergence-based approaches and the empirical gains are significant. The method does have many design choices which, while individually justified, could be explored more deeply, and some theoretical claims are only partially supported by the presented theory. The work makes a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to influence future research directions.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Wide-In, Narrow-Out: Revokable Decoding for Efficient and Effective DLLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18578v1)
- **Authors**: Feng Hong, Geng Yu, Yushi Ye, Haicheng Huang, Huangjie Zheng, Ya Zhang, Yanfeng Wang, Jiangchao Yao
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Large Language Models (DLLMs) have emerged as a compelling alternative to Autoregressive models, designed for fast parallel generation. However, existing DLLMs are plagued by a severe quality-speed trade-off, where faster parallel decoding leads to significant performance degradation. We attribute this to the irreversibility of standard decoding in DLLMs, which is easily polarized into the wrong decoding direction along with early error context accumulation. To resolve this, we introduce Wide-In, Narrow-Out (WINO), a training-free decoding algorithm that enables revokable decoding in DLLMs. WINO employs a parallel draft-and-verify mechanism, aggressively drafting multiple tokens while simultaneously using the model's bidirectional context to verify and re-mask suspicious ones for refinement. Verified in open-source DLLMs like LLaDA and MMaDA, WINO is shown to decisively improve the quality-speed trade-off. For instance, on the GSM8K math benchmark, it accelerates inference by 6$\times$ while improving accuracy by 2.58%; on Flickr30K captioning, it achieves a 10$\times$ speedup with higher performance. More comprehensive experiments are conducted to demonstrate the superiority and provide an in-depth understanding of WINO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Wide-In, Narrow-Out: Revokable Decoding for Efficient and Effective DLLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the quality-speed trade-off in Diffusion Large Language Models (DLLMs).  It argues that the irreversibility of standard decoding, where early errors become fixed, hinders performance when generating tokens in parallel for speed.  The authors propose Wide-In, Narrow-Out (WINO), a training-free decoding algorithm that enables revokable decoding. WINO uses a draft-and-verify mechanism, aggressively drafting multiple tokens in parallel ("Wide-In") and then using the model's bidirectional context to verify and re-mask ("Narrow-Out") suspicious tokens for refinement. They demonstrate improved performance on open-source DLLMs like LLaDA and MMaDA, showcasing speedups and accuracy improvements on various benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of "revokable decoding" in the context of DLLMs is novel. While draft-and-verify techniques have been explored in other domains, adapting it to the specific challenges of parallel token generation in DLLMs is a significant contribution. The WINO algorithm, including the specific design of the attention masks and the handling of shadow blocks for verification, also demonstrates novelty. The authors' attribution of the quality-speed trade-off to decoding irreversibility is not completely new, but their framing and solution are well-articulated.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in potentially unlocking the inherent parallelism of DLLMs without sacrificing quality. The experimental results showing speedups with accuracy *improvements* are compelling. If the technique proves generally applicable and scalable to larger models, it could represent a substantial advancement for the DLLM field, making them more competitive with autoregressive models in terms of both speed and accuracy. WINO is training-free and easily incorporated within existing DLLMs, further adding to its significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the quality-speed trade-off problem in DLLMs and provides a compelling rationale for its cause.
    *   **Well-Defined Solution:** The WINO algorithm is well-defined and explained, including the rationale for the design choices in the draft-and-verify process.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper provides comprehensive experimental results across a range of benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, Flickr30K, MMMU) and models (LLaDA, MMaDA). The reported speedups and accuracy improvements are substantial.
    *   **Ablation Studies and Analysis:** The ablation studies (e.g., removing the verification module, different generation lengths) and analyses (e.g., relation between speedup and task complexity, GPU memory usage, threshold tuning) provide valuable insights into the algorithm's behavior and robustness.
    *   **Case Studies:** The case studies showcasing the decoding dynamics provide a qualitative understanding of how WINO corrects early errors.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited to Open-Source Models:**  The experiments are limited to relatively smaller open-source models (LLaDA-8B and MMaDA-8B).  The real test of its significance will be its performance on much larger models comparable in scale to state-of-the-art autoregressive models or the closed-source DLLMs mentioned in the introduction. The authors acknowledge this limitation implicitly.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:**  While the paper mentions tuning the drafting threshold τ1, it doesn't provide a detailed analysis of its sensitivity or a robust method for automatically selecting it. The optimal value might vary across datasets and models, potentially requiring significant tuning effort.
    *   **Generality of the Approach:**  While the paper is training-free, it remains unclear if the approach generalizes across all types of DLLM architectures or if specific architectural adaptations might be required for optimal performance with WINO.
    *   **Lack of theoretical analysis:** The paper would benefit from a more formal, theoretical analysis of the WINO method.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the DLLM field by:
    *   Enabling faster and more accurate text generation using DLLMs.
    *   Making DLLMs more competitive with autoregressive models.
    *   Inspiring further research on revokable decoding and error correction techniques in non-autoregressive models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to address a critical limitation of DLLMs. The WINO algorithm is elegantly designed, and the experimental results are impressive. However, the limitations regarding the scale of the models tested and the potential hyperparameter sensitivity prevent a higher score. The true impact will be determined by its scalability and generalizability, areas requiring further investigation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DR.EHR: Dense Retrieval for Electronic Health Record with Knowledge Injection and Synthetic Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18583v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengyun Zhao, Huaiyuan Ying, Yue Zhong, Sheng Yu
- **Abstract**: Electronic Health Records (EHRs) are pivotal in clinical practices, yet their retrieval remains a challenge mainly due to semantic gap issues. Recent advancements in dense retrieval offer promising solutions but existing models, both general-domain and biomedical-domain, fall short due to insufficient medical knowledge or mismatched training corpora. This paper introduces \texttt{DR.EHR}, a series of dense retrieval models specifically tailored for EHR retrieval. We propose a two-stage training pipeline utilizing MIMIC-IV discharge summaries to address the need for extensive medical knowledge and large-scale training data. The first stage involves medical entity extraction and knowledge injection from a biomedical knowledge graph, while the second stage employs large language models to generate diverse training data. We train two variants of \texttt{DR.EHR}, with 110M and 7B parameters, respectively. Evaluated on the CliniQ benchmark, our models significantly outperforms all existing dense retrievers, achieving state-of-the-art results. Detailed analyses confirm our models' superiority across various match and query types, particularly in challenging semantic matches like implication and abbreviation. Ablation studies validate the effectiveness of each pipeline component, and supplementary experiments on EHR QA datasets demonstrate the models' generalizability on natural language questions, including complex ones with multiple entities. This work significantly advances EHR retrieval, offering a robust solution for clinical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DR.EHR, a series of dense retrieval models specifically designed for Electronic Health Record (EHR) retrieval.  It addresses the limitations of existing general and biomedical domain models by proposing a two-stage training pipeline. The first stage involves medical entity extraction and knowledge injection from a biomedical knowledge graph (BIOS), while the second stage utilizes large language models (LLMs) to generate diverse training data. Two variants of DR.EHR are trained (110M and 7B parameters) and evaluated on the CliniQ benchmark, where they significantly outperform existing dense retrievers and achieve state-of-the-art results.  The paper also includes detailed analyses demonstrating the models' superiority across various match and query types, particularly in challenging semantic matches.  Ablation studies validate the effectiveness of each pipeline component, and supplementary experiments show generalizability on natural language EHR QA datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Targeted Problem:** The paper tackles a crucial problem in clinical practice: efficient EHR retrieval. Existing methods, particularly exact match approaches, are demonstrably inadequate due to the semantic gap.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The two-stage training pipeline is a well-reasoned and novel approach. Knowledge injection from BIOS in the first stage effectively incorporates medical knowledge, and the synthetic data generation via LLMs in the second stage addresses the scarcity of labeled data. This combination is a key strength.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results on CliniQ are compelling. DR.EHR shows substantial and consistent improvements over strong baselines, including large proprietary models like OpenAI's text-embedding-3-large and NV-Embed. The detailed analysis across match types and query types adds further credence to the findings. The case studies vividly illustrate the model's advantages.
    *   **Thorough Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies convincingly demonstrate the importance of each component in the training pipeline, reinforcing the validity of the overall design.
    *   **Generalizability:** The supplementary experiments on EHR QA datasets, while not the core focus, provide evidence of the models' ability to handle more complex, natural language queries, strengthening the claim of generalizability.
    *   **Reproducibility**: The paper thoroughly specifies all the datasets and parameters, as well as providing the data allocation for each stage.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Data Generation:** The synthetic data generation, while a clever approach to address data scarcity, relies on the quality and potential biases of LLMs. Although they performed manual evaluations, a deeper analysis of potential biases introduced by LLMs in the synthetic data would strengthen the work.  The model still uses a large language model (LLM) to generate synthetic data. If the underlying LLM is biased, that data could be affected, which can lead to significant errors in the EHRs.
    *   **Limited Negative Sample Design:** While the paper uses in-batch negatives, the creation and utilization of more sophisticated *hard* negative samples could potentially further improve performance. The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Narrow focus on Single-Entity Queries:** The core evaluation on CliniQ focuses on single-entity retrieval. While the EHR QA experiments demonstrate broader capabilities, the primary performance gains are demonstrated on a relatively narrow task. This limits the assessment of the model's handling of more complex relationships and reasoning within EHRs.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The novelty primarily lies in the *combination* of knowledge injection and synthetic data generation, specifically tailored for the EHR retrieval task. While each technique is known, their synergistic application to overcome the challenges of EHR data is a significant contribution.
    *   The significance is high because effective EHR retrieval has direct clinical implications, improving efficiency, accuracy, and ultimately patient care. By substantially advancing the state-of-the-art in EHR retrieval, DR.EHR has the potential to impact clinical practice.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a significant problem in the medical domain. The strong empirical results, thorough ablation studies, and evidence of generalizability lend substantial weight to the claims. While the reliance on LLMs for synthetic data and the focus on single-entity retrieval represent limitations, the overall contribution is significant and addresses a pressing need in clinical practice. The work offers a robust solution to improve EHR retrieval and makes an important step toward bridging the semantic gap in EHR data. The combination of methods is original and clearly contributes to progress in the area. The model has been rigorously specified, so it would be easy for other scientists to reproduce this work.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### AQuilt: Weaving Logic and Self-Inspection into Low-Cost, High-Relevance Data Synthesis for Specialist LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18584v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaopeng Ke, Hexuan Deng, Xuebo Liu, Jun Rao, Zhenxi Song, Jun Yu, Min Zhang
- **Abstract**: Despite the impressive performance of large language models (LLMs) in general domains, they often underperform in specialized domains. Existing approaches typically rely on data synthesis methods and yield promising results by using unlabeled data to capture domain-specific features. However, these methods either incur high computational costs or suffer from performance limitations, while also demonstrating insufficient generalization across different tasks. To address these challenges, we propose AQuilt, a framework for constructing instruction-tuning data for any specialized domains from corresponding unlabeled data, including Answer, Question, Unlabeled data, Inspection, Logic, and Task type. By incorporating logic and inspection, we encourage reasoning processes and self-inspection to enhance model performance. Moreover, customizable task instructions enable high-quality data generation for any task. As a result, we construct a dataset of 703k examples to train a powerful data synthesis model. Experiments show that AQuilt is comparable to DeepSeek-V3 while utilizing just 17% of the production cost. Further analysis demonstrates that our generated data exhibits higher relevance to downstream tasks. Source code, models, and scripts are available at https://github.com/Krueske/AQuilt.
- **Summary**: ### Summary of the Paper The paper presents AQuilt, a novel framework aimed at improving the instruction-tuning process for specialized domains using low-cost data synthesis. AQuilt addresses existing limitations in data synthesis methodologies that often require high computational resources and struggle with generalization across tasks. The framework utilizes various components, including Answer, Question, Unlabeled data, Inspection, Logic, and Task type, to create 703k instruction-tuning examples from unlabeled data, thereby enhancing model performance through the incorporation of logic and self-inspection processes. The experimental results indicate that AQuilt performs comparably to DeepSeek-V3 while achieving only 17% of the associated production costs, demonstrating higher relevance for downstream tasks. The authors provide access to source code and relevant resources, making their work reproducible. --- ### Evaluation of Novelty and Significance #### Strengths: 1. **Cost Efficiency**: The significant reduction in production costs (to 17% of DeepSeek-V3) while maintaining comparable performance is a notable advancement, suggesting AQuilt can be more accessible for practitioners in specialized domains.     2. **Domain Adaptability**: The ability to generalize and customize instruction-tuning data generation across various specialized tasks positions AQuilt as a flexible tool that can cater to diverse application needs. 3. **Methodological Innovation**: By integrating self-inspection and logic into the data synthesis process, AQuilt encourages reasoning skills in LLMs that are often missing in traditional models, potentially leading to enhanced understanding and performance in complex tasks. 4. **Empirical Validation**: The creation of a substantial dataset (703k examples) and thorough experimental validation strengthens the paper's claims, showcasing the effectiveness of the proposed framework. #### Weaknesses: 1. **Comparative Analysis**: While the paper cites DeepSeek-V3 as a benchmark, it lacks an in-depth exploration of how AQuilt outperforms or addresses specific limitations of this and other related methods, which could better clarify its contributions. 2. **Scope of Generalization**: The effectiveness of AQuilt across a wider range of specialized domains needs further evaluation. The current experiments might not cover all aspects of domain complexity, leading to potential overgeneralization of results. 3. **Potential Oversights**: The reliance on logic and self-inspection could introduce complexities that may not always align with user needs or understanding, thus complicating the model's utility in certain contexts. 4. **Limited Real-World Validation**: While experiments offer promising results, real-world applications in varied settings have not been fully explored, leaving questions about the practicality of the approach. #### Overall Assessment: The notion of combining logic and self-inspection into data synthesis for specialized LLMs is innovative and addresses critical gaps in the current methodologies. By emphasizing both cost efficiency and performance relevance, AQuilt presents a meaningful contribution to the field. Nevertheless, there is a need for broader validation and a deeper comparative analysis with existing methodologies to fully appreciate its novel aspects and impact. Considering these factors, I assign the paper a score of **7**. It presents significant advancements and novel approaches that can potentially shape methodologies in the field, but further empirical support and clarification of its advantages over existing solutions would enhance its standing. **Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Linear Memory SE(2) Invariant Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18597v1)
- **Authors**: Ethan Pronovost, Neha Boloor, Peter Schleede, Noureldin Hendy, Andres Morales, Nicholas Roy
- **Abstract**: Processing spatial data is a key component in many learning tasks for autonomous driving such as motion forecasting, multi-agent simulation, and planning. Prior works have demonstrated the value in using SE(2) invariant network architectures that consider only the relative poses between objects (e.g. other agents, scene features such as traffic lanes). However, these methods compute the relative poses for all pairs of objects explicitly, requiring quadratic memory. In this work, we propose a mechanism for SE(2) invariant scaled dot-product attention that requires linear memory relative to the number of objects in the scene. Our SE(2) invariant transformer architecture enjoys the same scaling properties that have benefited large language models in recent years. We demonstrate experimentally that our approach is practical to implement and improves performance compared to comparable non-invariant architectures.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel SE(2) invariant attention mechanism for transformer architectures that requires linear memory, addressing the quadratic memory bottleneck of existing SE(2) invariant approaches. It leverages a Fourier series approximation to decompose the relative pose calculation into terms that can be computed with linear memory preprocessing, standard scaled dot-product attention (SDPA), and linear memory postprocessing. The proposed method (SE(2) Fourier) is shown to outperform comparable non-invariant and SE(2) representation approaches in an agent simulation task, exhibiting improved performance, particularly in handling turning trajectories.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the Fourier approximation technique for achieving linear memory complexity in an SE(2) invariant attention mechanism. This is a significant improvement over existing approaches that compute relative poses for all pairs, leading to quadratic memory demands. While the use of Fourier series isn't entirely new, its application to this specific problem and the resulting linear memory SE(2) attention is a notable contribution. The paper improves upon existing methods for incorporating relative positional information into transformers like RoPE and GTA, and provides a superior approach for modeling agent behavior with SE(2) invariance.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical benefits of reduced memory usage, enabling the scaling of SE(2) invariant transformers to larger scenes and more complex tasks. The demonstrated improvement in agent simulation performance, especially in turning trajectories, highlights the value of SE(2) invariance in such applications. The paper can have an impact on autonomous driving, robotics, and multi-agent simulation by enabling the training and deployment of more powerful SE(2) invariant models. The linear memory complexity makes it feasible to use these models in resource-constrained environments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the quadratic memory bottleneck of existing SE(2) invariant architectures.
    *   **Novel Solution:** The proposed Fourier approximation is a clever and effective technique for achieving linear memory complexity.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The agent simulation experiments provide empirical evidence of the performance benefits of the proposed method.
    *   **Detailed Mathematical Derivation:** The paper provides a rigorous mathematical derivation of the proposed method and the Fourier approximation.
    *   **Comparison to Existing Approaches:** The paper compares the proposed approach to existing relative pose and absolute position embedding techniques in a thorough manner.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Approximation Error:** The Fourier approximation introduces an approximation error, which could limit the performance of the model. Although the paper evaluates this error, further analysis of its impact on downstream tasks would be valuable. The approximation of the quadratic representation is not lossless; however, the performance gains demonstrated in the experimental section appear to demonstrate its viability.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the proposed method reduces memory usage, the computational cost of the Fourier approximation may be significant. Further analysis of the computational cost would be valuable, including a comparison to the computational cost of standard quadratic memory SE(2) invariant methods in different scenarios.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The experiments are primarily focused on agent simulation. Evaluating the proposed method on a wider range of tasks and datasets would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Potential Limitations of Fourier Series:** Fourier series are most effective for periodic functions. There is a potential limit as to how much an SE(2) pose can be approximated by a Fourier series, and this limitation could be discussed in greater detail.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of SE(2) invariant deep learning models for a variety of tasks. The linear memory complexity makes it feasible to use these models in resource-constrained environments, and the improved performance in agent simulation highlights the value of the proposed method. This could lead to improvements in autonomous driving, robotics, and multi-agent simulation.
    *   The method could act as a strong baseline for future SE(2) invariant linear attention research.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper introduces a significant and novel technique (Fourier series approximation) that makes SE(2) invariant attention mechanisms more scalable. The experimental results are promising, showing improved performance in agent simulation. However, there are some limitations regarding the approximation error, computational cost, and limited evaluation. Addressing these limitations in future work could further enhance the impact of the paper. Taking into account all of these factors, the paper merits a score of 7.5 due to its novelty, impact, and potential benefits to the broader field of robotics and autonomous driving. The score is greater than a 7 based on the potential of becoming a strong baseline for the aforementioned field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Demystify Protein Generation with Hierarchical Conditional Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18603v1)
- **Authors**: Zinan Ling, Yi Shi, Da Yan, Yang Zhou, Bo Hui
- **Abstract**: Generating novel and functional protein sequences is critical to a wide range of applications in biology. Recent advancements in conditional diffusion models have shown impressive empirical performance in protein generation tasks. However, reliable generations of protein remain an open research question in de novo protein design, especially when it comes to conditional diffusion models. Considering the biological function of a protein is determined by multi-level structures, we propose a novel multi-level conditional diffusion model that integrates both sequence-based and structure-based information for efficient end-to-end protein design guided by specified functions. By generating representations at different levels simultaneously, our framework can effectively model the inherent hierarchical relations between different levels, resulting in an informative and discriminative representation of the generated protein. We also propose a Protein-MMD, a new reliable evaluation metric, to evaluate the quality of generated protein with conditional diffusion models. Our new metric is able to capture both distributional and functional similarities between real and generated protein sequences while ensuring conditional consistency. We experiment with the benchmark datasets, and the results on conditional protein generation tasks demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed generation framework and evaluation metric.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of generating novel and functional protein sequences using conditional diffusion models.  Recognizing that protein function is determined by multi-level structures (sequence, backbone, all-atom), the authors propose a novel multi-level conditional diffusion model. This model integrates sequence-based and structure-based information to guide protein design towards specified functions.  The framework incorporates a rigid-body 3D rotation-invariant preprocessing step to maintain SE(3)-invariance.  The authors also introduce a new evaluation metric, Protein-MMD, based on Maximum Mean Discrepancy, to assess conditional consistency by capturing distributional and functional similarities between real and generated proteins.  Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed generation framework and evaluation metric.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits several novel aspects. The primary novelty lies in the multi-level conditional diffusion model tailored specifically for protein generation. While diffusion models have been applied to protein design previously, integrating sequence and structure information across multiple levels (amino acid, backbone, all-atom) with a conditional flow is a distinctive contribution. Also novel is the Protein-MMD, evaluation metric, which is designed specifically to tackle the unique challenges of conditional consistency evaluation in protein design, unlike more generic metrics like FID. The rigid-body 3D rotation-invariant preprocessing step is a helpful and perhaps necessary, though perhaps not individually ground breaking, addition.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the reliability and effectiveness of *de novo* protein design. Accurately generating functional protein sequences has broad applications in biotechnology and medicine. The proposed approach tackles the limitations of existing methods by considering multi-level structural dependencies and offering a more reliable metric for assessing conditional consistency. The experimental results, while promising, will need to be validated and extended by the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multi-Level Approach:**  The core idea of modeling protein structure at multiple levels is biologically plausible and potentially effective in capturing the complex relationships that govern protein function.
    *   **Conditional Consistency:** The introduction of Protein-MMD directly addresses a critical issue in conditional protein generation – ensuring that generated sequences adhere to specified functional constraints.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology is clearly presented, with detailed explanations of the multi-level diffusion process, conditional flow, and the Protein-MMD metric.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The authors provide experimental results on standard datasets, comparing their method with several baselines, which supports the claims.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Ablation Study:** While the ablation study is provided, it is relatively brief and primarily focuses on the impact of removing different levels. More detailed ablation studies to investigate the contribution of different components (e.g., rigid-body 3D rotation-invariant preprocessing) would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Protein-MMD limitations:** There are not enough validations or theoretical assurances (beyond the stated theorems), that Protein-MMD is truly superior in all cases, or what the failure modes would be.
    *   **Computational Cost:**  The paper does not thoroughly discuss the computational cost associated with the multi-level diffusion model. A comparison of training time and inference time with existing methods would be beneficial.
    *   **Real-World Validation:** The experiments are primarily conducted on benchmark datasets. Validation of the generated protein sequences through *in vitro* or *in vivo* experiments would significantly increase the impact of the work.
    *   **Lack of novelty in ESM2 usage:** The use of ESM2 as a backbone is a solid choice, but not novel.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in protein design and generative modeling. The multi-level approach and the Protein-MMD metric could become valuable tools for researchers in the field. The work could also spur further research into developing more reliable and efficient methods for generating functional protein sequences.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to a significant problem in protein design. The multi-level diffusion model and the Protein-MMD metric are promising contributions. However, the limitations in the ablation study, the lack of real-world validation, and the computational cost discussion temper enthusiasm.

Score: 7.0

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Explainable Mapper: Charting LLM Embedding Spaces Using Perturbation-Based Explanation and Verification Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18607v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyuan Yan, Rita Sevastjanova, Sinie van der Ben, Mennatallah El-Assady, Bei Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) produce high-dimensional embeddings that capture rich semantic and syntactic relationships between words, sentences, and concepts. Investigating the topological structures of LLM embedding spaces via mapper graphs enables us to understand their underlying structures. Specifically, a mapper graph summarizes the topological structure of the embedding space, where each node represents a topological neighborhood (containing a cluster of embeddings), and an edge connects two nodes if their corresponding neighborhoods overlap. However, manually exploring these embedding spaces to uncover encoded linguistic properties requires considerable human effort. To address this challenge, we introduce a framework for semi-automatic annotation of these embedding properties. To organize the exploration process, we first define a taxonomy of explorable elements within a mapper graph such as nodes, edges, paths, components, and trajectories. The annotation of these elements is executed through two types of customizable LLM-based agents that employ perturbation techniques for scalable and automated analysis. These agents help to explore and explain the characteristics of mapper elements and verify the robustness of the generated explanations. We instantiate the framework within a visual analytics workspace and demonstrate its effectiveness through case studies. In particular, we replicate findings from prior research on BERT's embedding properties across various layers of its architecture and provide further observations into the linguistic properties of topological neighborhoods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Explainable Mapper," a novel framework designed to explore and analyze the topological structure of Large Language Model (LLM) embedding spaces. The framework utilizes mapper graphs, a technique from topological data analysis, to represent these high-dimensional spaces. To assist in interpretation, the authors propose two types of customizable LLM-based agents: Explanation Agents and Verification Agents. Explanation Agents generate candidate explanations for mapper elements (nodes, edges, paths, etc.) using summarization, comparison, and perturbation operations. Verification Agents then assess the robustness of these explanations, helping users identify the most reliable insights. The framework is implemented in a visual analytics workspace, and its effectiveness is demonstrated through replication studies of prior research on BERT embeddings. The goal is to provide a semi-automatic approach to annotating embedding properties, making it easier to uncover encoded linguistic features and verify the generated explanations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the application of a specific combination of existing techniques (mapper graphs and LLM-based agents) to the problem of interpreting LLM embeddings. Using mapper graphs for embedding analysis isn't entirely new, but the integration of customizable explanation and verification agents to explore the space systematically is a valuable contribution. The agent framework allows a more structured and scalable way to annotate the different elements of a mapper graph.

*   **Significance:** Understanding how LLMs encode linguistic information is crucial for model development, bias detection, and improving the interpretability of AI systems. This paper addresses this need by providing a framework to chart LLM embeddings more effectively. The potential impact on the field includes:

    *   **Improved LLM Interpretability:** The framework makes it easier for researchers and practitioners to gain insights into the complex representations learned by LLMs.
    *   **Enhanced Model Debugging:** By identifying linguistic properties encoded in embedding spaces, the method can help uncover potential biases or limitations of LLMs.
    *   **Facilitating Knowledge Discovery:** The interactive workspace allows exploring and verifying hypotheses, enabling researchers to discover new relationships between linguistic features and embedding structures.
    *   **Structured Automation**: Offers a way to use LLMs in a more structured manner and provide human experts with better insights to extract information from the embedding vectors

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The paper provides a clear and comprehensive description of the Explainable Mapper framework, including its components, operations, and usage workflow.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The framework is implemented in a visual analytics workspace, making it accessible and user-friendly.
    *   **Demonstrated Effectiveness:** Replication studies validate the framework's ability to reproduce prior research findings.
    *   **Careful handling of LLM properties:** The incorporation of both "Explanation Agents" (for hypotheses creation) and "Verification Agents" (for hypothesis confirmation) is important for dealing with the inherent hallucination risks of LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework's performance heavily relies on the quality and reliability of the LLMs used for explanation and verification. LLM hallucinations are mentioned, but the mitigation strategies may not be sufficient to eliminate all inaccuracies. The paper mentions that, it mainly addresses the explanation, but it doesn't guarantee absolute correctness.
    *   **Limited Scalability Assessment:** While the framework is designed to be scalable, the paper could benefit from a more thorough evaluation of its performance on extremely large and complex embedding spaces.
    *   **User-Dependent trajectory creation:** The Trajectory verifier is user-dependent, which is not following the scalable and automated approach.
    *   **Modifications in trajectory analysis**: As the algorithm makes some decision based on the "key difference between nodes", it can lead to biases. The use of precise words to look for key differences may have a bias.
    *   **Limited scope: The authors limit their experiments to the BERT model**

*   **Potential Influence:** The framework has the potential to become a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners working with LLMs. By providing a structured and scalable approach to interpreting embedding spaces, it can accelerate research in areas such as model understanding, bias detection, and linguistic analysis.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM interpretability. The integration of mapper graphs with LLM-based agents offers a novel and practical approach to exploring embedding spaces. The framework's well-defined design, implementation in a user-friendly workspace, and demonstrated effectiveness through replication studies make it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners. While some limitations exist, the paper addresses an important challenge and has the potential to significantly impact the field by facilitating a deeper understanding of LLM representations. The systematic automation is important. The scalability challenges and the reliance on LLM accuracy are the reason that the rating isn't higher.

- **Classification**: cs.CG
- **Score**: 7/10

### TRPrompt: Bootstrapping Query-Aware Prompt Optimization from Textual Rewards
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18618v1)
- **Authors**: Andreea Nica, Ivan Zakazov, Nicolas Mario Baldwin, Saibo Geng, Robert West
- **Abstract**: Prompt optimization improves the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs) without requiring parameter updates to the target model. Following heuristic-based "Think step by step" approaches, the field has evolved in two main directions: while one group of methods uses textual feedback to elicit improved prompts from general-purpose LLMs in a training-free way, a concurrent line of research relies on numerical rewards to train a special prompt model, tailored for providing optimal prompts to the target model. In this paper, we introduce the Textual Reward Prompt framework (TRPrompt), which unifies these approaches by directly incorporating textual feedback into training of the prompt model. Our framework does not require prior dataset collection and is being iteratively improved with the feedback on the generated prompts. When coupled with the capacity of an LLM to internalize the notion of what a "good" prompt is, the high-resolution signal provided by the textual rewards allows us to train a prompt model yielding state-of-the-art query-specific prompts for the problems from the challenging math datasets GSMHard and MATH.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the TRPrompt paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRPrompt, a framework for optimizing prompts for large language models (LLMs) in a query-dependent manner. TRPrompt distinguishes itself by using *textual* rewards, rather than numerical rewards, to guide the training of a prompt model. This prompt model generates query-specific instructions that are prepended to questions, aiming to improve the target LLM's reasoning abilities.  The framework iteratively refines the prompt model through a process of synthetic data generation, supervised fine-tuning, and an optimal reward search step. Experiments on mathematical reasoning datasets (GSM8K, GSMHard, and MATH) demonstrate TRPrompt's effectiveness, achieving state-of-the-art performance on the more challenging datasets and showing the ability to learn effective prompts from scratch without relying on handcrafted initial prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The key novelty of TRPrompt lies in its *direct* integration of textual rewards into the training loop of a prompt optimization model.  Previous work has explored using textual feedback for improving reward models themselves (for RLHF) or in train-free methods that refine prompts iteratively, but TRPrompt uniquely employs textual rewards as the primary supervisory signal for training a specialized prompt generation model. This is a significant departure from existing numerical reward-based query-dependent prompt optimization techniques. While prior works used the output of LLMs for generating the textual rewards, the paper designs a process that enables the LLM to learn from its own limitations during the iterative process, creating better prompts.

*   **Significance:**  The use of textual rewards addresses a critical limitation in query-dependent prompt optimization: the difficulty of designing effective numerical rewards that capture the nuanced quality of a prompt. Textual rewards offer richer, more informative feedback, potentially leading to better prompt optimization and improved reasoning abilities in LLMs.  The experimental results, particularly the state-of-the-art performance on GSMHard and MATH, support this claim. The ability to generate good prompts without expert knowledge by just using textual feedback has important implications to the field and might facilitate the application to areas were automatic metrics are not very informative.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The framework is well-defined and clearly explained.
    *   The iterative nature of the training process is a key strength, allowing the prompt model to learn from its past mistakes.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple datasets and comparing against strong baselines.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of each component of the framework.
    *   The cross-dataset transfer experiments highlight the generalization capabilities of TRPrompt.
    *   The approach is model-agnostic and does not require prior dataset collection, making it highly adaptable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper mentions the high computational cost of the Optimal Reward Search step (using Textgrad) as a bottleneck.  This limits scalability and hinders training on larger datasets.
    *   The performance gains on the simpler GSM8K dataset are modest, suggesting that textual rewards may not be as beneficial when the target model already performs well. This could be due to the limited diversity of textual feedback when most prompts already lead to correct answers.
    *   The reliance on Textgrad for optimal reward update, while effective, introduces a dependence on a separate LLM (GPT-4o-mini). A more integrated approach could be beneficial.
    *   The framework has not been tested on an extensive variety of problems or with different types of LLMs, so its scope and applicability is still limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** TRPrompt has the potential to influence future research in prompt optimization and LLM alignment. It highlights the value of textual rewards and provides a practical framework for leveraging them. This could lead to the development of more sophisticated prompt optimization techniques that are better suited to complex reasoning tasks. The use of iterative self-improvement via textual rewards also opens interesting avenues for exploration in other areas of LLM research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of prompt engineering. The approach of directly incorporating textual rewards into the training of a query-dependent prompt model is a notable advancement over existing methods. The state-of-the-art results on challenging mathematical reasoning tasks provide strong evidence of the effectiveness of TRPrompt.  While the high computational cost and the limited gains on simpler datasets are valid concerns, the overall novelty, significance, and potential influence of the work justify a high score. It opens the way to a broader field in prompt engineering where textual feedback might be incorporated and fine-tuned to adapt the model to different user behaviours.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Captain Cinema: Towards Short Movie Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18634v1)
- **Authors**: Junfei Xiao, Ceyuan Yang, Lvmin Zhang, Shengqu Cai, Yang Zhao, Yuwei Guo, Gordon Wetzstein, Maneesh Agrawala, Alan Yuille, Lu Jiang
- **Abstract**: We present Captain Cinema, a generation framework for short movie generation. Given a detailed textual description of a movie storyline, our approach firstly generates a sequence of keyframes that outline the entire narrative, which ensures long-range coherence in both the storyline and visual appearance (e.g., scenes and characters). We refer to this step as top-down keyframe planning. These keyframes then serve as conditioning signals for a video synthesis model, which supports long context learning, to produce the spatio-temporal dynamics between them. This step is referred to as bottom-up video synthesis. To support stable and efficient generation of multi-scene long narrative cinematic works, we introduce an interleaved training strategy for Multimodal Diffusion Transformers (MM-DiT), specifically adapted for long-context video data. Our model is trained on a specially curated cinematic dataset consisting of interleaved data pairs. Our experiments demonstrate that Captain Cinema performs favorably in the automated creation of visually coherent and narrative consistent short movies in high quality and efficiency. Project page: https://thecinema.ai
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Captain Cinema paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Captain Cinema," a framework for generating short movies from detailed textual descriptions. The approach employs a two-stage process: "top-down keyframe planning" where a sequence of keyframes is generated to outline the narrative and ensure long-range coherence, followed by "bottom-up video synthesis," which uses these keyframes as conditioning signals to generate the video and spatio-temporal dynamics.  The core innovation is handling long-context data efficiently and maintaining visual and narrative consistency across multiple scenes, achieved through an interleaved training strategy for Multimodal Diffusion Transformers (MM-DiT) and a memory mechanism called "GoldenMem."

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The concept of combining top-down keyframe planning with bottom-up video synthesis is a reasonable approach to tackling the problem of long-form video generation. The "GoldenMem" mechanism for compressing long-context visual memory is a potential contribution. The hybrid attention masking and dynamic stride sampling appear as relevant ideas. However, the reliance on existing models like Flux and Seaweed-3B as building blocks diminishes the core architectural novelty. Many parts of the approach appear to be using already established techniques, and the paper does not offer a groundbreaking methodology.
* **Significance:**  Automated long-form video generation is a significant challenge with a broad range of potential applications. Achieving narrative coherence and visual consistency over extended durations is a hard problem, and Captain Cinema attempts to address it directly. Successfully generating even short movies with coherent narratives could have a noticeable impact. However, the provided qualitative results aren't exceedingly compelling and don't immediately suggest a substantial advance over existing video generation methods. The quantitative results also lack a strong margin against the baseline.
* **Strengths:**
    * The two-stage approach of keyframe planning and video synthesis is a logical way to handle long-range coherence.
    * Addressing the exploding context length problem with GoldenMem is a practical consideration.
    * Demonstrating some degree of generalisation abilities (creative scene generation and cross-movie character swapping) expands possibilities.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The qualitative results, while showing some coherence, are not visually stunning and appear somewhat generic. It would be useful to compare the method directly with prior art.
    * The quantitative gains over baselines (LCT, IC-LORA + I2V) aren't overwhelmingly large, and in some metrics the performance is only marginally better.
    * The paper depends on a specific set of pre-existing models. Reliance on proprietary models isn't ideal.
    * The long-context generation uses Gemini 2.5 for rating visual aspects, and these results depend highly on LLM and its performance.
* **Potential Influence:**  The paper can influence researchers to explore memory mechanisms for long-form video generation. It could also spur further development of techniques for interleaved keyframe planning and conditioning for video synthesis. However, the limited impact of the quantitative results and the reliance on existing architecture reduce the potential for groundbreaking impact.

**Justification for the Score:**

Captain Cinema tackles a significant problem in video generation. The approach is logical and includes some practical innovations. However, the dependence on existing models, relatively small quantitative gains, and qualitatively limited results prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The paper provides an incremental improvement over existing methods, but it does not offer a paradigm shift or a significant leap forward in the field.
